<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For this glutamyl-tRNA reductase, which assignment of functions to its three domains is correct?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> The N-terminal domain binds NADPH, the second domain mediates dimerization, and the C-terminal domain recognizes the glutamate moiety.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> The N-terminal domain mediates dimerization, the second domain recognizes the glutamate moiety, and the C-terminal domain binds NADPH.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> The N-terminal domain recognizes the glutamate moiety, the second domain mediates dimerization, and the C-terminal domain binds NADPH.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> The N-terminal domain recognizes the glutamate moiety, the second domain binds NADPH, and the C-terminal domain mediates dimerization.<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: Glutamyl-tRNA reductase (GluTR/HemA) is a well-characterized enzyme in the tRNA-dependent chlorophyll biosynthesis pathway. Structural and biochemical studies of this enzyme (particularly in E. coli) reveal a three-domain architecture:<br><br>- **N-terminal domain (~residues 1-200)**: This is the catalytic domain containing a conserved active-site cysteine that recognizes and binds the glutamate moiety of glutamyl-tRNA, the substrate for the reduction reaction.<br><br>- **Second domain (~residues 200-370)**: This domain contains a Rossmann fold that binds the NADPH cofactor. Evidence from the sequence includes the characteristic glycine-rich motif &quot;GAGKMG&quot; (a GxGxxG pattern typical of NADPH-binding Rossmann folds), which appears in the central region of the protein.<br><br>- **C-terminal domain (~residues 370-450)**: This domain mediates homodimerization, as GluTR functions as a homodimer.<br><br>The sequence length (~445 residues) and the presence of the Rossmann fold motif in the middle region support this assignment. The N-terminal region contains the catalytic machinery for substrate recognition, the middle region binds the cofactor, and the C-terminal region provides the dimerization interface.<br><br>This corresponds to option D.<br><br>2. answer: D</td>

3. C-terminal domain (approximately residues 370-450): This domain is involved in dimerization. GluTR functions as a homodimer, and this C-terminal domain mediates the dimerization.

Actually, let me reconsider again. Looking at structural studies of GluTR:

From structural studies of E. coli GluTR (HemA):
- The N-terminal domain (residues ~1-200) contains the catalytic/substrate-binding domain that recognizes the glutamate moiety.
- The central domain (residues ~200-370) contains a Rossmann-fold for NADPH binding.
- The C-terminal domain (residues ~370-450) mediates dimerization.

This matches option D: The N-terminal domain recognizes the glutamate moiety, the second domain binds NADPH, and the C-terminal domain mediates dimerization.

The N-terminal region contains the catalytic cysteine and recognizes the glutamate substrate.

The C-terminal region is involved in dimerization.

So the answer is D.

Not a proton donor in catalytic mechanisms.<br>- **Aspartate (D) &amp; Glutamate (B):** Their side chains CAN participate in acid-base chemistry (they have pKa values ~3.9 and ~4.3, respectively). However, in active sites they are typically selected for roles as **metal ligands**, **nucleophiles**, or **charge-stabilizing residues** — not specifically as proton donors. Their low pKa makes them poor proton donors at physiological pH.<br>- **Histidine (C):** Its imidazole side chain has a pKa (~6.0) near physiological pH, making it uniquely and specifically suited to **donate and accept protons** during catalysis. Histidine is the classic, purpose-selected proton donor in enzyme active sites (as seen in catalytic triads of serine proteases, histidine kinases, and many metalloenzymes). It is selected *because* of its proton-donor capability, not merely because it could do acid-base chemistry.<br><br>The distinction is clear: His is chosen *for* the proton-donor role; Asp/Glu could do it but are chosen for other purposes.<br><br>2. answer: C</td>
